    55	class Alert(v.Alert, SepalWidget):
    56	    """
    57	    A custom Alert widget. It is used as the output of all processes in the framework.
    58	    In the voila interfaces, print statement will not be displayed.
    59	    Instead use the sw.Alert method to provide information to the user.
    60	    It's hidden by default.
    61	
    62	    Args:
    63	        type\_ (str, optional): The color of the Alert
    64	        kwargs (optional): any parameter from a v.Alert. If set, 'type' will be overwritten.
    65	    """
    66	
    67	    def __init__(self, type_="info", **kwargs):
    68	
    69	        # set default parameters
    70	        kwargs["text"] = kwargs.pop("text", True)
    71	        kwargs["type"] = set_type(type_)
    72	        kwargs["class_"] = kwargs.pop("class_", "mt-5")
    73	
    74	        # call the constructor
    75	        super().__init__(**kwargs)
    76	
    77	        self.hide()
    78	        self.progress_output = Output()
    79	        self.progress_bar = None
    80	
    81	    def update_progress(self, progress, msg="Progress", **tqdm_args):
    82	        """
    83	        Update the Alert message with a tqdm progress bar.
    84	
    85	        Args:
    86	            progress (float): the progress status in float [0, 1]
    87	            msg (str, optionnal): The message to use before the progress bar
    88	
    89	        Return:
    90	            self
    91	        """
    92	
    93	        # show the alert
    94	        self.show()
    95	
    96	        # cast the progress to float
    97	        progress = float(progress)
    98	        if not (0 <= progress <= 1):
    99	            raise ValueError(f"progress should be in [0, 1], {progress} given")
   100	
   101	        # Prevent adding multiple times
   102	        if self.progress_output not in self.children:
   103	
   104	            self.children = [self.progress_output]
   105	
   106	            tqdm_args["bar_format"] = tqdm_args.pop(
   107	                "bar_format", "{l_bar}{bar}{n_fmt}/{total_fmt}"
   108	            )
   109	            tqdm_args["dynamic_ncols"] = tqdm_args.pop("dynamic_ncols", tqdm_args)
   110	            tqdm_args["total"] = tqdm_args.pop("total", 100)
   111	            tqdm_args["desc"] = tqdm_args.pop("desc", msg)
   112	            tqdm_args["colour"] = tqdm_args.pop("tqdm_args", getattr(color, self.type))
   113	
   114	            with self.progress_output:
   115	                self.progress_output.clear_output()
   116	                self.progress_bar = tqdm(**tqdm_args)
   117	                self.progress_bar.container.children[0].add_class(f"{self.type}--text")
   118	                self.progress_bar.container.children[2].add_class(f"{self.type}--text")
   119	
   120	                # Initialize bar
   121	                self.progress_bar.update(0)
   122	
   123	        self.progress_bar.update(progress * 100 - self.progress_bar.n)
   124	
   125	        if progress == 1:
   126	            self.progress_bar.close()
   127	
   128	        return
   129	
   130	    def add_msg(self, msg, type_="info"):
   131	        """
   132	        Add a message in the alert by replacing all the existing one.
   133	        The color can also be changed dynamically
   134	
   135	        Args:
   136	            msg (str): the message to display
   137	            type\_ (str, optional): the color to use in the widget
   138	
   139	        Return:
   140	            self
   141	        """
   142	        self.show()
   143	        self.type = set_type(type_)
   144	        self.children = [v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg])]
   145	
   146	        return self
   147	
   148	    def add_live_msg(self, msg, type_="info"):
   149	        """
   150	        Add a message in the alert by replacing all the existing one.
   151	        Also add the timestamp of the display.
   152	        The color can also be changed dynamically
   153	
   154	        Args:
   155	            msg (str): the message to display
   156	            type\_ (str, optional): the color to use in the widget
   157	
   158	        Return:
   159	            self
   160	        """
   161	
   162	        current_time = datetime.now().strftime("%Y/%m/%d, %H:%M:%S")
   163	
   164	        self.show()
   165	        self.type = set_type(type_)
   166	        self.children = [
   167	            v.Html(tag="p", children=["[{}]".format(current_time)]),
   168	            v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg]),
   169	        ]
   170	
   171	        return self
   172	
   173	    def append_msg(self, msg, section=False, type_="info"):
   174	        """
   175	        Append a message in a new parragraph, with or without divider
   176	
   177	        Args:
   178	            msg (str): the message to display
   179	            section (bool, optional): add a Divider before the added message
   180	
   181	        Return:
   182	            self
   183	        """
   184	
   185	        self.show()
   186	        self.type = type_
   187	
   188	        if len(self.children):
   189	            current_children = self.children[:]
   190	            if section:
   191	                # As the list is mutable, and the trait is only triggered when
   192	                # the children is changed, so have to create a copy.
   193	                divider = Divider(class_="my-4", style="opacity: 0.22")
   194	
   195	                # link Alert type with divider type
   196	                directional_link((self, "type"), (divider, "type_"))
   197	                current_children.extend([divider, v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg])])
   198	                self.children = current_children
   199	
   200	            else:
   201	                current_children.append(v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg]))
   202	                self.children = current_children
   203	        else:
   204	            self.add_msg(msg)
   205	
   206	        return self
   207	
   208	    def remove_last_msg(self):
   209	        """
   210	        Remove the last msg printed in the Alert widget
   211	
   212	        Return:
   213	            self
   214	        """
   215	
   216	        if len(self.children) > 1:
   217	            current_children = self.children[:]
   218	            self.children = current_children[:-1]
   219	        else:
   220	            self.reset()
   221	
   222	        return self
   223	
   224	    def reset(self):
   225	        """
   226	        Empty the messages and hide it
   227	
   228	        Return:
   229	            self
   230	        """
   231	
   232	        self.children = [""]
   233	        self.hide()
   234	
   235	        return self

import sys
from collections import deque

def main():
    n = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    p = list(map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split()))
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]  # 1-based indexing
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        parent = p[i - 1]
        if parent != 0:
            adj[i].append(parent)
            adj[parent].append(i)
    degrees = [len(neighbors) for neighbors in adj]
    q = deque()
    processed = [False] * (n + 1)
    result = []
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if degrees[i] % 2 == 0:
            q.append(i)
    while q:
        u = q.popleft()
        if processed[u] or degrees[u] % 2 != 0:
            continue
        result.append(u)
        processed[u] = True
        for v in adj[u]:
            if not processed[v]:
                degrees[v] -= 1
                if degrees[v] % 2 == 0:
                    q.append(v)
    if len(result) == n:
        print("YES")
        print('\n'.join(map(str, result)))
    else:
        print("NO")

if __name__ == "__main__":

</think>To solve the problem, we start by noting that in right triangle \( \triangle ABC \) with \( \angle ABC = 90^\circ \), the hypotenuse \( AC \) is the diameter of the circumcircle. The length of \( AC \) is calculated using the Pythagorean theorem:

\[
AC = \sqrt{AB^2 + BC^2} = \sqrt{16^2 + 30^2} = \sqrt{256 + 900} = \sqrt{1156} = 34
\]

The circumradius is \( \frac{AC}{2} = 17 \), and the circumcircle is centered at the midpoint \( O \) of \( AC \), which has coordinates \( (15, 8) \).

The point \( P \) is the midpoint of the arc \( AC \) not containing \( B \). Since \( B \) is at \( (0, 0) \), the arc not containing \( B \) is the upper semicircle. The coordinates of \( P \) are found by rotating the vector from \( O \) to \( A \) (which is \( (-15, 8) \)) 90 degrees clockwise around \( O \). This gives the point \( (23, 23) \).

Next, we determine the coordinates of points \( X \) and \( Y \). Since \( PX \perp AB \) (vertical line), \( X \) lies on the line \( AB \) (the y-axis) at \( (0, 23) \). Similarly, since \( PY \perp BC \) (horizontal line), \( Y \) lies on the line \( BC \) (the x-axis) at \( (23, 0) \).

The distance \( XY \) is calculated as follows:

\[
XY^2 = (23 - 0)^2 + (0 - 23)^2 = 23^2 + 23^2 = 2 \times 23^2 = 2 \times 529 = 1058
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{1058}

   255	class StateBar(v.SystemBar, SepalWidget):

   267	    "bool: either or not the circular progress is spinning"
   268	
   269	    progress = None
   270	    "widget: The ProgressCircular widget that will be displayed in the statebar"
   271	
   272	    def __init__(self, **kwargs):
   273	
   274	        self.progress = v.ProgressCircular(
   275	            indeterminate=self.loading,
   276	            value=100,
   277	            small=True,
   278	            size=15,
   279	            color="primary",
   280	            class_="mr-2",
   281	        )
   282	
   283	        # set default parameter
   284	        kwargs["children"] = [self.progress, self.msg]
   285	
   286	        # call the constructor
   287	        super().__init__(**kwargs)
   288	
   289	        jslink((self, "loading"), (self.progress, "indeterminate"))
   290	
   291	    @observe("loading")
   292	    def _change_loading(self, change):
   293	        """Change progress wheel state"""
   294	        self.progress.indeterminate = self.loading
   295	
   296	    @observe("msg")
   297	    def _change_msg(self, change):
   298	        """Change state bar message"""
   299	        self.children = [self.progress, self.msg]
   300	
   301	    def add_msg(self, msg, loading=False):
   302	        """Change current status message"""
   303	        self.msg = msg
   304	        self.loading = loading
   305	
   306	        return self
   307	
   308	
   309	class Banner(v.Snackbar, SepalWidget):
   310	    """
   311	    Custom Snackbar widget to display messages as a banner in module App.
   312	
   313	    Args:
   314	       msg (str, optional): Message to display in application banner. default to nothing
   315	       type\_ (str, optional): Used to display an appropiate banner color. fallback to "info".
   316	       id_ (str, optional): unique banner identificator.
   317	       persistent (bool, optional): Whether to close automatically based on the lenght of message (False) or make it indefinitely open (True). Overridden if timeout duration is set.
   318	       kwargs (optional): any parameter from a v.Alert. If set, 'vertical' and 'top' will be overwritten.
   319	    """
   320	
   321	    btn_close = None
   322	    "v.Btn: the closing btn of the banner"
   323	
   324	    def __init__(self, msg="", type_="info", id_=None, persistent=True, **kwargs):
   325	
   326	        # compute the type and default to "info" if it's not existing
   327	        type_ = set_type(type_)
   328	
   329	        # create the closing btn
   330	        self.btn_close = v.Btn(
   331	            small=True, text=True, children=[ms.widgets.banner.close]
   332	        )
   333	
   334	        # compute timeout based on the persistent and timeout parameter
   335	        computed_timeout = 0 if persistent is True else self.get_timeout(msg)
   336	
   337	        kwargs["color"] = kwargs.pop("color", type_)
   338	        kwargs["transition"] = kwargs.pop("transition", "scroll-x-transition")
   339	        kwargs["attributes"] = {"id": id_}
   340	        kwargs["v_model"] = kwargs.pop("v_model", True)
   341	        kwargs["timeout"] = kwargs.pop("timeout", False) or computed_timeout
   342	        kwargs["top"] = True
   343	        kwargs["vertical"] = True
   344	        kwargs["children"] = [msg] + [self.btn_close]
   345	        kwargs["class_"] = "mb-1"
   346	
   347	        super().__init__(**kwargs)
   348	
   349	        self.btn_close.on_event("click", self.close)
   350	
   351	    def close(self, *args):
   352	        """Close button event to close snackbar alert"""
   353	        self.v_model = False
   354	
   355	        return
   356	
   357	    def get_timeout(self, text):
   358	        """
   359	        Calculate timeout in miliseconds to read the message
   360	
   361	        Args:
   362	            text (str): the text displayed in the banner to adapt the duration of the timeout
   363	
   364	        Returns
   365	            (int): the duration of the timeout in milliseconds
   366	        """
   367	
   368	        wpm = 180  # readable words per minute
   369	        word_length = 5  # standardized number of chars in calculable word
   370	        words = len(text) / word_length
   371	        words_time = ((words / wpm) * 60) * 1000
   372	
   373	        delay = 1500  # milliseconds before user starts reading the notification
   374	        bonus = 1000  # extra time
   375	
   376	        return delay + words_time + bonus
   377	
   378	    def set_btn(self, nb_banner):
   379	        """
   380	        Change the btn display to inform the user on the number of banners in the queue
   381	
   382	        Args:
   383	            nb_banner (int): the number of banners in the queue
   384	        """
   385	        # do not wrap ms.widget.banner. If you do it won't be recognized by the key-checker of the Translator
   386	        if nb_banner == 0:
   387	            txt = ms.widgets.banner.close
   388	        else:
   389	            txt = ms.widgets.banner.next.format(nb_banner)
   390	        self.btn_close.children = [txt]
   391	
   392	        return
   393

n, m, k = map(int, input().split())
s = list(map(int, input().split()))
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

blocked_ptr = 0
available = []
for x in range(n):
    if blocked_ptr < m and x == s[blocked_ptr]:
        blocked_ptr += 1
    else:
        available.append(x)

if not available:
    print(-1)
else:
    min_cost = float('inf')
    import bisect
    for l in range(1, k+1):
        current = 0
        cnt = 0
        possible = True
        prev_current = -1
        while current < n:
            if current == prev_current:
                possible = False
                break
            prev_current = current
            idx = bisect.bisect_right(available, current) - 1
            if idx < 0:
                possible = False
                break
            x = available[idx]
            new_current = x + l
            if new_current <= current:
                possible = False
                break
            current = new_current
            cnt += 1
        if possible and current >= n:
            cost = a[l-1] * cnt
            if cost < min_cost:
                min_cost = cost

    20	def get_templates():
    21	    """Get all the templates files from the templates and save them in the distribution"""

Thus, if we can precompute a binary array indicating which nodes are in the set {1..current k}, then for any node B (root of the subtree), we can compute how many nodes in the subset are in its subtree.

Interestingly, this can be done with Binary Indexed Trees (Fenwick trees) or segment trees, but since the nodes are numbered arbitrarily, and the subset is 1..k, we need to find an ordering based on subtree.

Ah, but the only way a count in a subtree can be quickly queried is to process nodes in a certain order such as entrance and exit times in a DFS traversal.

Thus, if the tree has been processed with a DFS to assing an in-time and out-time to each node which is the start and end when the subtree is visited, then the number of nodes in a subtree rooted at B is the number of nodes in 1..k whose in-time is between in[B] and out[B].

If the in-time ordering allows this, then we can use a prefix tree or a segment tree with a frequency array, which allows this range query.

Alternatively, we can process the nodes in an Euler Tour numbering so that all nodes in a subtree are contiguous in the in-time.

With this setup, the subtree count for node B would be equal to the number of elements in {1..k} where in_time of x is between in_time[B] and out_time[B], which can be done via a prefix sum array.

Therefore:

The algorithm could be as follows:

Preprocessing steps:

1. Perform a DFS (or BFS) to assign in_time and out_time to each node, such that all nodes in the subtree rooted at B have in_time between in_time[B] and out_time[B].

2. Create an array in_time of size n, where in_time[i] is the entrance time for node i.

3. Sort the in_time array's indices, so that for any set of nodes, we can map them to their in_time and query how many are in a range.

T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N, M = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    B = list(map(int, input().split()))
    i = j = 0
    merged = []
    while i < N and j < M:
        if A[i] >= B[j]:
            merged.append(A[i])
            i += 1
        else:
            merged.append(B[j])
            j += 1
    merged.extend(A[i:])
    merged.extend(B[j:])
    print(' '.join(map(str, merged)))<#sep#>9k² -6k -2=0. Same as before, leading to irrational roots. So even if one root is 1/3 and another is integer, the third root would be irrational. Hence, this approach doesn't help.

Alternatively, perhaps all roots are fractions with denominator 3. Let’s assume a = d/3, b = e/3, c = f/3 where d, e, f are integers. Then:

a + b + c = (d + e + f)/3 =1 ⇒ d + e + f =3.

ab + ac + bc = (de + df + ef)/9 =0 ⇒ de + df + ef =0.

Product abc = (def)/27 = -m/3 ⇒ m = -def/9.

Since m must be integer, def must be divisible by 9. So def ≡0 mod9.

We need to find integers d, e, f such that d + e + f=3, de + df + ef=0, and def is divisible by9.

Let me try to find such integers.

Since d + e + f =3 and de + df + ef=0.

Let me consider possible triples (d, e, f) that sum to 3.

First, possible integer solutions. Let me try small integers.

Case 1: All three are 1: 1 +1 +1=3. Then de + df + ef=1+1+1=3≠0. Not good.

Case 2: 3,0,0: 3+0+0=3. Then de + df + ef=0+0+0=0. So de + df + ef=0. Okay. So this works. Then def=0, so m = -0/9=0. Which we already found as m=0.

Case3: 2,1,0: 2+1+0=3. de + df + ef=2*1 +2*0 +1*0=2≠0. Not good.

Case4: 4, -1,0:4 +(-1)+0=3. de + df + ef=4*(-1) +4*0 + (-1)*0= -4≠0.

Case6: 6, -3,0:6 +(-3) +0=3. de + df + ef=6*(-3) +6*0 + (-3)*0= -18≠0.

So even though the sum and product conditions are satisfied, m isn't integer here. So this case is invalid.

Case9: What about 3, -3, 3: sum 3. de + df + ef=3*(-3) +3*3 + (-3)*3= -9 +9 -9=-9≠0.

Case10: Let's think of other triples. Let's try 6, 3, -6: sum 6+3-6=3. de + df + ef=6*3 +6*(-6)+3*(-6)=18 -36 -18= -36≠0.

Hmm. Not helpful.

Suppose d = e. Then we have 2d + f =3. Also, de + df + ef = d² + 2df =0.

From the first equation: f =3 -2d.

Substitute into second equation: d² + 2d*(3 -2d)=0 ⇒ d² +6d -4d²=0 ⇒ -3d² +6d=0 ⇒ -3d(d -2)=0 ⇒ d=0 or d=2.

If d=0, then f=3 -0=3. So the triple is (0,0,3). Which we already considered, leading to m=0.

If d=2, then f=3 -4= -1. So triple (2,2,-1). Which we considered in case8, which gives m=4/9, not integer.

So no other solutions here.

Alternatively, what if all three numbers are different.

Wait, maybe there's another approach. Let's look for triples (d,e,f) such that d + e + f=3 and de + df + ef=0. Let me denote S=3, P= de + df + ef=0. Alternatively, let me consider that:

(d + e + f)^2 = d² + e² + f² + 2(de + df + ef) = 9. But de + df + ef=0, so d² + e² + f²=9.

Therefore, the sum of squares of the integers d, e, f is 9. Since they are integers, what triples of integers have squares summing to 9?

Possible combinations:

±3,0,0: 9 +0 +0=9.

±2, ±2, ±1: 4 +4 +1=9.

Case1: (3,0,0): sum is 3. Squares sum to 9. This works. We already considered this.

Wait, so the only integer triples that satisfy d + e + f=3 and d² + e² + f²=9 are (3,0,0) and permutations, and (2,2,-1) and permutations. The first gives m=0, the second gives m=4/9 (invalid). Therefore, only m=0.

But the problem says "all solutions are rational". So if m=0, then yes. But is there any other m?

Wait, maybe other m where the polynomial factors with rational roots, even if not all roots are integers. For example, maybe roots are fractions but all rational. Let me check.

Suppose the polynomial factors as (3x² + ax + b)(x + c) where a, b, c are integers. Then expanding:

3x³ + (a + 3c)x² + (ac + b)x + bc = 3x³ -3x² + m. So comparing coefficients:

1. Leading coefficient: 3x³, okay.

2. x² term: a + 3c = -3.

3. x term: ac + b = 0 (since there<#sep#> an array (sorted) where each entry holds (in_time of node, node_number).

Sort this array by in_time. 

Then, the function range_query(x) = count of numbers (node's index) ≤ s (since new subset is 1..s) and in in_time[x] ≤ in_time[vertex] ≤ in_time[x_end].

However, because the in_time[x] and out_time[x] define the interval, and we want the indices only up to s.

This requires a 2D query, which is tricky with standard structures.

Alternative approach: For each target s (current total nodes k+1), we can track all the nodes in the subset l to s (the current node being added now).

We need to do this for any s up to n.

We can process and maintain a structure which can quickly tell, for a given x's interval [L, R], how many of the nodes in 1 to s have in_time in [L, R].

This can be represented with a Fenwick Tree indexed by in_time.

For example, we can have a Fenwick Tree where each position holds a 1 if its corresponding in_time is for a node in the subset (i<= s). 

Then, for a node x:

the number of nodes in the subset within x's subtree is query_Fenwick(out_time[x]) - query_Fenwick(in_time[x] -1).

Wait, but how:

Assuming the in_time are unique and can be sorted. Let me think of it as:

Fenwick Tree holds a boolean (presence or not) for each node's in_time. 

But in_time could be arbitrary. So need to map in_time to a numerical position.

Suppose we take the in_times of all nodes in an array, sort them, and assign each in_time a rank. 

Let T be the list of in_time of all nodes, sorted in increasing order. 

For each in_time value, the corresponding rank is its position in T.

The Fenwick Tree is of size N, indexed by rank. 

Then, when adding node x (node's number), which has in_time of t, we do Fenwick_tree.update( (rank(t)), +1).

Then, for querying the number of nodes up to s with in_time in [A,B], it can be:

find the number of in_time values less than or equal to current s's nodes (i.e. nodes with number from1 to s) whose in_time is between L and R.

Wait maybe it's better to treat the Fenwick Tree as a frequency array where for each node i (1..n), initially it's 0. 

When a node j is added (i.e when k reaches j), we perform Fenwick_tree.update(node_in_time[j]'s rank, 1).

Then, the query for a node x's subtree [in_x, out_x] would be:

the number of active nodes up to s is the sum of Fenwick_tree in the in_time interval [in[x], out[x]], which can be computed as upper = out[x], lower = in[x]: Fenwick.query(rank(upper)) - Fenwick.query(rank(lower) -1).

This would require:

- Pre-sort in_time to get their ranks.

- Each in_time mapped to its rank.

- All Fenwick operations use the rank as index.

This way, the number can be computed with two Fenwick queries.

Therefore, the preprocess steps are:

1. Compute in_time and out_time arrays via DFS.

2. Collect all in_time in an array, sort it to get a ranking.

3. For each node, compute its rank in this sorted list.

4. Initialize a Fenwick Tree of size N.

For the algorithm:

We'll manage the Fenwick Tree incrementally. For k from1 to n:

- When processing k (to advance to k+1):

   - Before considering k+1, the current subset is 1..k. 

   Therefore to handle the next step (k+1), current step is relating to adding the k+1th node.

Wait, let me think:

Initially, for k=1, the subset is 1..1.

The Fenwick Tree is initialized with node 1's in_time added.

Each time we process the increment to add k+1, the next steps are:

   - Add node u = (k+1): update the Fenwick Tree with its in_time.

   - Now the subset is 1..k+1.

   - Find the path between previous optimal v and node u =k+1.

   - Along this path from v to u, we can compute for each edge the number of nodes in subtree of the child node (the direction towards u), via m = Fenwick.query( child's interval).

   - Then for each edge along the path, decide whethermoving to the child is better than staying, or not.

   - Move towards the node that gives better sum until the threshold is passed.

The minimal sum is achieved precisely after stopping at the edge where further movement degrades the total.

However, this requires:

   a. Traversal of the path between v and u,

   b. For each edge along the path, compute m (number of nodes in subset in the child's subtree),

   c. determine whether stepping towards the child node improves the sum.

The algorithm needs to find where along this path the minimal sum is obtained.

The key is how to traverse the path and compute the necessary m's efficiently.

Computing the path between two nodes can be done with LCA.

The standard method is to find LCA of v and u, then compute the upward path from v to LCA, and then the downward path from LCA to u.

Thus, the edges along the path can be decomposed into these two segments.

To compute the edges between current v and u:

1. Find LCA of v and u.

   - Let L be the LCA.

   - Path from v to L: go up, then from L down to u.

Thus the path is v → parent(v) → ... → L → ... → child(L) → ... → u.

The distance from v to u's path can be partitioned into two paths: up to L, then down to u.

Now, for each edge on this path from v to u, moving step by step towards u, we can compute for each such edge the m value (count in subset's nodes in child's direction) and check whether taking this step is beneficial.

This way we can walk along the path, until the marginal improvement becomes negative.

But how do I do this computation:

At each edge along the path (starting from v towards u via LCA path):

   When considering edge from current position x to next node y (along the path):

   - Compute m as the number of nodes in the subset which are in the subtree of y.

   (the part of the path is moving from LCA towards u, so y is along the direction to 'u')

   - The number of nodes not in the subtree is total nodes so far (s =k+1) minus m.

   - The delta for moving to y is:

     delta = (m * distance - ( (s - m) )*distance )

     Wait distance between moving along that edge is the distance between x and y, say d(x,y).

     The total delta here is (m*-d(x,y)) + (s-m)*d(x,y)), 

     wait, the contribution where the new total delta is:

     per each node in the subtree (m: the nodes in the subtree are getting closer, so their contribution reduces. Conversely, nodes not in the subtree are further away, so increase.

     The total distance change from x to y is: 

          for all m nodes in the subtree of y: distance decreases by d(x,y) 

          for the other (s -m) nodes: distance increases by d(x,y).

          So:

     change = (-m*d + ( (s - m) ) *d ) 

     = ( (s -m -m) *d ) 

     = (s -2m) *d 

             (because moving to y improves the total by (s-2m)*d. but since we are trying to minimize, this is the delta:

             the difference in the sum when we move to y is (sum after movement) minus (sum before) = delta = (s-2m)*d.

             So if delta is negative, moving to y reduces the sum.)

             thus, if (s-2m) <0 → s<2m: then moving is better.

        So, while moving along the path to u until m becomes as large as possible and the delta is still negative, 

        we can move along. 

The optimal point is where this delta is positive, so we have to roll back.

Suppose moving further edges gives m increasing further. 

Hence, once (s-2*m) becomes ≥0, it is no longer beneficial. 

Hence the optimal stepping is up to that edge. 

Therefore, the path can be traversed, edge by edge,<#sep#>―――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――― TestAlert.test_update_progress ――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.read().split()))
    ptr = 0
    N, Q = data[ptr], data[ptr+1]
    ptr += 2
    A = data[ptr:ptr+N]
    ptr += N
    queries = data[ptr:ptr+Q]
    s = set(A)
    for x in queries:
        print("YES" if x in s else "NO")

Suppose the polynomial factors as (3x² + ax + b)(x + c) where a, b, c are integers. Then expanding:

3x³ + (a + 3c)x² + (ac + b)x + bc = 3x³ -3x² + m. So comparing coefficients:

1. Leading coefficient: 3x³, okay.

2. x² term: a + 3c = -3.

3. x term: ac + b = 0 (since there is no x term in the original polynomial; coefficient is 0).

4. Constant term: bc = m.

So we have a system:

a + 3c = -3,

ac + b = 0,

bc = m.

We can solve this system for integers a, b, c.

From the first equation, a = -3 - 3c.

Substitute into second equation: (-3 -3c)c + b = 0 ⇒ -3c -3c² + b =0 ⇒ b = 3c +3c².

From the third equation, bc = m. Substituting b:

(3c +3c²)c = m ⇒ 3c² +3c³ = m.

So m = 3c³ +3c² =3c²(c +1).

So m must be of the form 3c²(c +1) where c is an integer. So for any integer c, m is 3c²(c +1). Then the possible m are multiples of 3c²(c +1). Since c is integer, let's compute m for different integer values of c.

Let me try c=0: m=3*0²*(0 +1)=0. Which we already have.

c=1: m=3*1²*(1+1)=3*1*2=6.

c=-1: m=3*(-1)²*(-1 +1)=3*1*0=0.

c=2: m=3*4*(3)=36.

c=-2: m=3*4*(-2 +1)=12*(-1)=-12.

c=3: m=3*9*4=108.

c=-3: m=3*9*(-3 +1)=27*(-2)=-54.

So possible m values are 0, 6, -12, 36, -54, 108, etc., based on different c. But wait, the problem asks for all integer m such that all solutions are rational. So if the polynomial factors as (3x² + ax +b)(x +c) with integer a, b, c, then all roots would be rational. But we need to ensure that both factors have rational roots. The quadratic factor 3x² +ax +b must also have rational roots. So the discriminant of the quadratic must be a perfect square.

So for the quadratic 3x² +ax +b, discriminant D = a² - 12b must be a perfect square.

But since a = -3 -3c and b =3c +3c², as per earlier, substitute:

D = (-3 -3c)² -12*(3c +3c²) = 9 +18c +9c² -36c -36c² =9 + (18c -36c) + (9c² -36c²)=9 -18c -27c²=9(1 -2c -3c²).

So D=9( -3c² -2c +1 ). For D to be a perfect square, the expression inside must be a perfect square divided by 9, but more precisely, since D is 9 times (-3c² -2c +1), and D must be a perfect square. Let me write this as D=9*( -3c² -2c +1 ). Let me factor out the 9:

D =9*(-3c² -2c +1). Therefore, -3c² -2c +1 must be a perfect square (since 9 is already a square). Let me denote k² = -3c² -2c +1.

We need integers c such that -3c² -2c +1 is a perfect square. Let's check for different c values.

Let's check c=0: -0 -0 +1=1=1². So yes, k=±1. So c=0 is acceptable.

c=1: -3 -2 +1=-4. Not a square.

c=-1: -3*1 -2*(-1) +1 =-3 +2 +1=0=0². So k=0. So acceptable.

c=2: -3*4 -4 +1=-12-4+1=-15. Not square.

c=-2: -3*4 -(-4) +1= -12 +4 +1=-7. Not square.

c=3: -3*9 -6 +1=-27-6+1=-32. Not square.

c=-3: -3*9 -(-6) +1= -27 +6 +1=-20. Not square.

c=4: -3*16 -8 +1=-48-8+1=-55. Not square.

c=-4: -3*16 -(-8) +1= -48 +8 +1=-39. Not square.

So only c=0 and c=-1 yield D as a perfect square.

For c=0:

Then m=3*0²*(0+1)=0. Which we already know.

For c=-1:

m=3*(-1)^2*(-1 +1)=3*1*0=0. Again, m=0.

So even though there are other m's like 6, -12, etc., the quadratic factor would not have rational roots because their discriminants aren't perfect squares. Therefore, those m's do not result in all roots being rational.

Wait, so even if we consider factoring into linear and quadratic terms, the only valid c's are c=0 and c=-1, both leading to m=0. So m=0 is the only solution.

But earlier, when we considered c=1, m=6, but the discriminant D would be -4, which is not a perfect square, so quadratic factor would have irrational roots, hence the original equation would have irrational roots, so m=6 is invalid. Similarly for other c's.

Therefore, the only integer m for which all roots are rational is m=0.

But wait, let me check when c=-1:

If c=-1, then from above:

a = -3 -3*(-1)= -3 +3=0,

b=3*(-1) +3*(-1)^2= -3 +3*1=0,

</think>To find all integers \( m \) such that all solutions of the equation \( 3x^3 - 3x^2 + m = 0 \) are rational numbers, we proceed as follows:

1. **Using Vieta's Formulas:**
   Let the roots of the polynomial \( 3x^3 - 3x^2 + m = 0 \) be \( a, b, \) and \( c \). By Vieta's formulas, we have:
   \[
   a + b + c = 1,
   \]
   \[
   ab + ac + bc = 0,
   \]
   \[
   abc = -\frac{m}{3}.
   \]

2. **Rational Root Theorem:**
   According to the Rational Root Theorem, any rational root \( \frac{p}{q} \) must have \( p \) dividing the constant term \( m \) and \( q \) dividing the leading coefficient \( 3 \). Therefore, the possible rational roots are \( \pm 1, \pm \frac{1}{3} \).

3. **Checking Integer Roots:**
   Suppose all roots are integers. We need to find integers \( a, b, \) and \( c \) such that:
   \[
   a + b + c = 1,
   \]
   \[
   ab + ac + bc = 0,
   \]
   \[
   abc = -\frac{m}{3}.
   \]
   Testing possible integer combinations:
   - If \( a = 0 \), \( b = 0 \), and \( c = 1 \), then:
     \[
     0 + 0 + 1 = 1,
     \]
     \[
     0 \cdot 0 + 0 \cdot 1 + 0 \cdot 1 = 0,
     \]
     \[
     0 \cdot 0 \cdot 1 = 0 \implies m = 0.
     \]

4. **Checking Rational Roots:**
   Suppose one or more roots are fractions. Let's consider the possibility of one root being \( \frac{1}{3} \):
   - If \( a = \frac{1}{3} \), then \( b + c = \frac{2}{3} \). The equation \( ab + ac + bc = 0 \) becomes:
     \[
     \frac{1}{3}b + \frac{1}{3}c + bc = 0 \implies \frac{1}{3}(b + c) + bc = 0 \implies \frac{1}{3} \cdot \frac{2}{3} + bc = 0 \implies \frac{2}{9} + bc = 0 \implies bc = -\frac{2}{9}.
     \]
     This implies that \( b \) and \( c \) are irrational, which contradicts the requirement that all roots are rational.

5. **Factoring the Polynomial:**
   Suppose the polynomial can be factored into linear terms:
   \[
   3(x - a)(x - b)(x - c).
   \]
   Expanding this, we get:
   \[
   3x^3 - 3(a + b + c)x^2 + 3(ab + ac + bc)x - 3abc.
   \]
   Comparing coefficients with \( 3x^3 - 3x^2 + m \), we have:
   \[
   a + b + c = 1,
   \]
   \[
   ab + ac + bc = 0,
   \]
   \[
   abc = -\frac{m}{3}.
   \]
   The only integer solution that satisfies all these conditions is \( a = 0 \), \( b = 0 \), and \( c = 1 \), leading to \( m = 0 \).

6. **Conclusion:**
   After verifying all possible cases, the only integer \( m \) such that all solutions of the equation \( 3x^3 - 3x^2 + m = 0 \) are rational is:

<think>
Okay, so I have this problem where I need to find all polynomials P(x, y) such that for all real numbers x and y, P(x, y) equals P(x+1, y) and also equals P(x, y+1). Hmm, interesting. Let me try to figure this out step by step.

First, let me understand what the problem is asking. We need a polynomial in two variables, x and y, that doesn't change its value when we increase either x or y by 1. So, adding 1 to x or y doesn't affect the value of the polynomial. That seems like the polynomial has to be periodic with period 1 in both variables. But wait, polynomials are usually not periodic unless they're constant. Wait, but maybe there's an exception here?

Wait, let's think about polynomials. A single-variable polynomial that's periodic must be constant. Because, for example, if you have a non-constant polynomial, its growth as x increases would make it impossible to be periodic. But here we have a two-variable polynomial. But the same reasoning might apply? Let me check.

Suppose P(x, y) is a non-constant polynomial. Then, if we fix y and vary x, it's a polynomial in x. If it's periodic in x with period 1, then this polynomial in x must be constant. Similarly, if we fix x and vary y, the polynomial in y must also be constant. So does this mean that P(x, y) must be a constant polynomial?

Wait, but maybe there's a way for the polynomial to depend on x and y in such a way that when you shift x or y by 1, the changes cancel out. But polynomials can't really do that unless the variables are wrapped somehow, but polynomials aren't typically periodic. Hmm.

Alternatively, maybe the polynomial is constant in x and y. Let me test with an example. Suppose P(x, y) = c, where c is a constant. Then certainly, P(x+1, y) = c = P(x, y), and similarly for y. So constant polynomials work. Are there any non-constant polynomials that satisfy this?

Let's see. Suppose P(x, y) is not constant. Then, consider the difference P(x+1, y) - P(x, y). By the given condition, this difference must be zero for all x and y. But the difference of two polynomials is another polynomial. So, if the difference is identically zero, that would mean that the polynomial is invariant under shifting x by 1. Similarly for shifting y by 1.

But how can a non-constant polynomial have such a property? Let me think. Let's take a simple case. Suppose P(x, y) is linear in x. So P(x, y) = a(y)x + b(y), where a(y) and b(y) are polynomials in y. Then, P(x+1, y) = a(y)(x+1) + b(y) = a(y)x + a(y) + b(y). For this to equal P(x, y) = a(y)x + b(y), we need a(y) = 0. So the polynomial can't have any x terms. Similarly, if we consider linear in y, we get the same result. So linear terms are out.

What if it's quadratic? Let's say P(x, y) = c x y + d x + e y + f. Then, P(x+1, y) = c(x+1)y + d(x+1) + e y + f = c x y + c y + d x + d + e y + f. Compare to original: c x y + d x + e y + f. The difference is c y + d. For this to be zero for all x and y, coefficients of y and the constant term must be zero. So c = 0 and d = 0. So that reduces to P(x, y) = e y + f. But then, if we check P(x, y+1) = e(y+1) + f = e y + e + f. This should equal e y + f, so e must be zero. Hence, only constant term f remains. So quadratic also doesn't work.

Wait, maybe higher degree terms? Let's try a monomial, say x^n y^m. Then P(x+1, y) = (x+1)^n y^m. For this to equal x^n y^m for all x, y, we need (x+1)^n y^m = x^n y^m. Dividing both sides by y^m (assuming y ≠ 0), we get (x+1)^n = x^n. But this must hold for all x. However, (x+1)^n - x^n is a polynomial of degree n-1. The only way for this to be zero for all x is if n = 0, i.e., the monomial is constant. Similarly, for y^m, shifting y to y+1 would require (y+1)^m = y^m, which only holds for m=0.

Therefore, any monomial with a positive degree in x or y would not satisfy the periodicity condition. Thus, the only monomial that works is the constant term. Hence, the only polynomial that satisfies the condition is a constant polynomial.

But wait, let me check if there could be a polynomial that combines x and y in some way that the shifts cancel. For example, maybe something like sin(π x) + sin(π y), but those aren't polynomials. Polynomials can't have periodicity unless they are constants, as their terms are monomials of non-negative integer exponents, which are not periodic.

Alternatively, maybe considering polynomials in variables that are periodic. But in the problem statement, the variables are x and y, real numbers. The polynomial is in x and y, so unless the polynomial is constant, there's no way for it to be periodic in x or y. Because adding 1 to x or y would increase the value of any non-constant monomial, unless the exponent is zero. Therefore, the only possible solution is a constant polynomial.

Therefore, the answer is that all such polynomials are constant polynomials.

Wait, but the problem says "find all polynomials". So the answer is that P(x, y) is constant. Let me just verify once more.

Suppose P is a non-constant polynomial. Then, there exists at least one term with degree ≥1. Suppose the term is x^k y^m, with k + m ≥1. Then shifting x by 1 gives (x+1)^k y^m. Expanding (x+1)^k, we get x^k + ... + 1. So when subtracted from x^k y^m, the difference would be ( ... + lower degree terms ) y^m, which is a non-zero polynomial. Hence, unless k=0, that term would violate the condition. Similarly for m. Therefore, all exponents must be zero. Hence, only constant terms survive.

Therefore, the conclusion is that the only polynomials satisfying the conditions are constants. So P(x, y) = C, where C is a constant.

Cubax is composed of N³ smaller cubes (elements) linked together in a kind of doubly linked chain. The chain has two ends, it doesn't loop.
3 successive elements of Cubax are either:

always aligned
always at a right angle (90°)

Thus a simple way of defining Cubax's structure is to provide the successive number of aligned blocks, starting from the first element.
For example, representing S as the starting elements, E as the ending element and X as other elements, the structure defined as 332 could take any of the following 2D shapes:
  XE   EX
  X     X
SXX   SXX   SXX   SXX
              X     X
             EX     XE

Another example: 434 could be represented as:
S E
X X
X X
XXX

Note: Counting the successive the number of aligned elements implies that elements in an angle are counted twice. In the last example 434, there are actually only 4+3-1+4-1=9 elements.
To provide the output solution, use the following rules.
For each series of aligned blocks, one character representing the direction where it has to point to is written:

U Direction Up (increasing y)
D Direction Down (decreasing y)
R Direction Right (increasing x)
L Direction Left (decreasing x)
F Direction Front (increasing z)
B Direction Back (decreasing z)

Considering a 3D representation (x,y,z), we will only be interested in the solutions where the first element is located at (1,1,1) and where the last element is located at (N,N,N)
Note: As a consequence, there will be no valid solution starting by D, L or B.
Example
For the input 3332, the output RDRD corresponds to the following planar shape:
SXX
  X
  XXX
    E

For the same input 3332, the output RDLU corresponds to:
SXX
E X
XXX

Input & Output
Your program has to read the initial shape of Cubax and provide the moves to apply so as to pack it back to a full cube of side length N.
You are given an integer N, the side length of the cube, and a string that contains a string of Blocks -> each character providing the successive number of aligned blocks
You are to output the alphabetically-sorted list of all solutions, one solution per line
Test cases:

RFLUBRF

UFDRBUF
URDFLUR

# 3*3*3 cube

3
232333232223222233
->
FRFLUBRFDRBLBRDRUF
FUFDRBUFLUBDBULURF
RFRBULFRDFLBLFDFUR
RURDFLURBULDLUBUFR
UFUBRDFULFDBDFLFRU
URULFDRUBRDLDRBRFU

# 4*4*4 cube

4
3232242323232222222233222223422242322222222242
->
FRBULFDRBUBDBRFUFDFULBUBRDRULDFURDFLULDBLFUBRF
FUBRDFLUBRBLBUFRFLFRDBRBULURDLFRULFDRDLBDFRBUF
RFLUBRDFLULDLFRURDRUBLULFDFUBDRUFDRBUBDLBRULFR

UFDRBULFDRDLDFURULURBDRDFLFRBLURFLUBRBLDBURDFU
URDFLUBRDFDBDRUFUBUFLDFDRBRFLBUFRBULFLBDLUFDRU

Others:

You are allowed to output the moves in a string or list/array

You are allowed to input the aligned blocks numbers via list/array as well

You may assume there will always be at least 1 solution to the puzzle

You are allowed to take in the input as 2 lines or on the same line as a list/array/string representation

n = int(input())
prefixes = []
suffixes = []
max_streaks = []
is_full_list = []

for _ in range(n):
    s = input().strip()
    # Compute prefix
    prefix = 0
    for c in s:
        if c == 'C':
            prefix += 1
        else:
            break
    prefixes.append(prefix)
    
    # Compute suffix
    suffix = 0
    for c in reversed(s):
        if c == 'C':
            suffix += 1
        else:
            break
    suffixes.append(suffix)
    
    # Compute max streak
    max_streak = 0
    current = 0
    for c in s:
        if c == 'C':
            current += 1
            if current > max_streak:
                max_streak = current
        else:
            current = 0
    max_streaks.append(max_streak)
    is_full_list.append(max_streak == 1440)

# Compute X
X = max(max_streaks)

# Compute prefix sums for full days
prefix_sum = [0] * (n + 1)
for i in range(n):
    prefix_sum[i+1] = prefix_sum[i] + (1 if is_full_list[i] else 0)

Y = X

# Check all possible pairs (i, j)
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(i+1, n):
        start = i + 1
        end = j - 1
        if start > end:
            full_days = 0
            required = 0
        else:
            full_days = prefix_sum[end + 1] - prefix_sum[start]
            required = end - start + 1
        if full_days == required:
            chain = suffixes[i] + (j - i - 1) * 1440 + prefixes[j]
            if chain > Y:
                Y = chain

print(X, Y)<#sep#> \( P(x, y) = P(x+1, y) \).**
   - For \( P(x, y) \) to be periodic in \( x \) with period 1, we must have:
     \[
     P(x+1, y) = \sum_{i=0}^n \sum_{j=0}^m a_{ij} (x+1)^i y^j = P(x, y).
     \]
   - Expanding \( (x+1)^i \) using the binomial theorem:
     \[
     (x+1)^i = \sum_{k=0}^i \binom{i}{k} x^k.
     \]
   - Substituting this into the polynomial:
     \[
     P(x+1, y) = \sum_{i=0}^n \sum_{j=0}^m a_{ij} \left( \sum_{k=0}^i \binom{i}{k} x^k \right) y^j.
     \]
   - For \( P(x+1, y) \) to equal \( P(x, y) \), the coefficients of corresponding powers of \( x \) and \( y \) must match. This implies that the higher-order terms in \( x \) must cancel out, which is only possible if all coefficients \( a_{ij} \) for \( i > 0 \) are zero.

3. **Analyze the periodicity condition \( P(x, y) = P(x, y+1) \).**
   - Similarly, for \( P(x, y) \) to be periodic in \( y \) with period 1, we must have:
     \[
     P(x, y+1) = \sum_{i=0}^n \sum_{j=0}^m a_{ij} x^i (y+1)^j = P(x, y).
     \]
   - Expanding \( (y+1)^j \) using the binomial theorem:
     \[
     (y+1)^j = \sum_{k=0}^j \binom{j}{k} y^k.
     \]
   - Substituting this into the polynomial:
     \[
     P(x, y+1) = \sum_{i=0}^n \sum_{j=0}^m a_{ij} x^i \left( \sum_{k=0}^j \binom{j}{k} y^k \right).
     \]
   - For \( P(x, y+1) \) to equal \( P(x, y) \), the coefficients of corresponding powers of \( x \) and \( y \) must match. This implies that the higher-order terms in \( y \) must cancel out, which is only possible if all coefficients \( a_{ij} \) for \( j > 0 \) are zero.

4. **Combine the results.**
   - From both conditions, we conclude that all coefficients \( a_{ij} \) for \( i > 0 \) and \( j > 0 \) must be zero. Therefore, the only remaining term is the constant term \( a_{00} \).

5. **Conclusion.**
   - The polynomial \( P(x, y) \) must be a constant polynomial. Let \( C \) be this constant.

Thus, the solution is:
\[
\boxed{P(x, y) = C \text{, where } C \text{ is a constant.}}
\]<#sep#> So first direction must be U, R, F.

import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    t = int(data[0])
    res = []
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(t):
        n = int(data[idx])
        k = int(data[idx + 1])
        idx += 2
        if (n - 1) % (k + 1) == 0:
            res.append("Nandu")
        else:
            res.append("Rani")
    print('\n'.join(res))

So the plan is to implement a backtracking algorithm with the following steps:

1. Parse the input segments into an array (segment_lengths). Let's say the segments array is S = [s1, s2, ..., sk]

2. The total number of directions needed is k (the length of S), so each direction must correspond to each segment.

3. The state in backtracking will include:

- current_position (x,y,z)
- previous direction's axis (since the next must choose different axis)
- visited set (so path can’t cross itself)
- the direction sequence so far (list of chars)
- which index in the segments is being handled next (i)

4. The base case is that when i reaches len(segments), we have to check that current_position == (N,N,N), and all steps have been taken. Then, if yes, then the direction sequence is part of the solution.

First, if i == 0 (first segment):

- Must be in the first direction. The allowed initial directions are those in positive axes (R, U, F). So directions R, U, F.

Else:

- The current direction must have an axis different from the previous direction's axis.

Additionally:

Once D is selected, we check:

- The axis of direction D must not be same as previous.

    57	    A custom Alert widget. It is used as the output of all processes in the framework.

    65	    """
    66	
    67	    def __init__(self, type_="info", **kwargs):
    68	
    69	        # set default parameters
    70	        kwargs["text"] = kwargs.pop("text", True)
    71	        kwargs["type"] = set_type(type_)
    72	        kwargs["class_"] = kwargs.pop("class_", "mt-5")
    73	
    74	        # call the constructor
    75	        super().__init__(**kwargs)
    76

    78	        self.progress_output = Output()

    80	
    81	    def update_progress(self, progress, msg="Progress", **tqdm_args):
    82	        """
    83	        Update the Alert message with a tqdm progress bar.
    84	
    85	        Args:
    86	            progress (float): the progress status in float [0, 1]
    87	            msg (str, optionnal): The message to use before the progress bar
    88	
    89	        Return:
    90	            self
    91	        """
    92	
    93	        # show the alert
    94	        self.show()
    95	
    96	        # cast the progress to float
    97	        progress = float(progress)
    98	        if not (0 <= progress <= 1):
    99	            raise ValueError(f"progress should be in [0, 1], {progress} given")
   100	
   101	        # Prevent adding multiple times
   102	        if self.progress_output not in self.children:
   103	
   104	            self.children = [self.progress_output]
   105	
   106	            tqdm_args["bar_format"] = tqdm_args.pop(
   107	                "bar_format", "{l_bar}{bar}{n_fmt}/{total_fmt}"
   108	            )
   109	            tqdm_args["dynamic_ncols"] = tqdm_args.pop("dynamic_ncols", tqdm_args)
   110	            tqdm_args["total"] = tqdm_args.pop("total", 100)
   111	            tqdm_args["desc"] = tqdm_args.pop("desc", msg)
   112	            tqdm_args["colour"] = tqdm_args.pop("tqdm_args", getattr(color, self.type))
   113	
   114	            with self.progress_output:
   115	                self.progress_output.clear_output()
   116	                self.progress_bar = tqdm(**tqdm_args)
   117	                self.progress_bar.container.children[0].add_class(f"{self.type}--text")
   118	                self.progress_bar.container.children[2].add_class(f"{self.type}--text")
   119	
   120	                # Initialize bar
   121	                self.progress_bar.update(0)
   122	
   123	        self.progress_bar.update(progress * 100 - self.progress_bar.n)
   124	
   125	        if progress == 1:
   126	            self.progress_bar.close()
   127

   135	        Args:
   136	            msg (str): the message to display
   137	            type\_ (str, optional): the color to use in the widget
   138	
   139	        Return:
   140	            self
   141	        """
   142	        self.show()
   143	        self.type = set_type(type_)
   144	        self.children = [v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg])]
   145	
   146	        return self
   147	
   148	    def add_live_msg(self, msg, type_="info"):
   149	        """
   150	        Add a message in the alert by replacing all the existing one.
   151	        Also add the timestamp of the display.
   152	        The color can also be changed dynamically
   153	
   154	        Args:
   155	            msg (str): the message to display
   156	            type\_ (str, optional): the color to use in the widget
   157	
   158	        Return:
   159	            self
   160	        """
   161	
   162	        current_time = datetime.now().strftime("%Y/%m/%d, %H:%M:%S")
   163	
   164	        self.show()
   165	        self.type = set_type(type_)
   166	        self.children = [
   167	            v.Html(tag="p", children=["[{}]".format(current_time)]),
   168	            v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg]),
   169	        ]
   170	
   171	        return self
   172	
   173	    def append_msg(self, msg, section=False, type_="info"):
   174	        """
   175	        Append a message in a new parragraph, with or without divider
   176	
   177	        Args:
   178	            msg (str): the message to display
   179	            section (bool, optional): add a Divider before the added message
   180	
   181	        Return:
   182	            self
   183	        """
   184	
   185	        self.show()
   186	        self.type = type_
   187	
   188	        if len(self.children):
   189	            current_children = self.children[:]
   190	            if section:
   191	                # As the list is mutable, and the trait is only triggered when
   192	                # the children is changed, so have to create a copy.
   193	                divider = Divider(class_="my-4", style="opacity: 0.22")
   194	
   195	                # link Alert type with divider type
   196	                directional_link((self, "type"), (divider, "type_"))
   197	                current_children.extend([divider, v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg])])
   198	                self.children = current_children
   199	
   200	            else:
   201	                current_children.append(v.Html(tag="p", children=[msg]))
   202	                self.children = current_children
   203	        else:
   204	            self.add_msg(msg)
   205	
   206	        return self
   207	
   208	    def remove_last_msg(self):
   209	        """
   210	        Remove the last msg printed in the Alert widget
   211	
   212	        Return:
   213	            self
   214	        """
   215	
   216	        if len(self.children) > 1:
   217	            current_children = self.children[:]
   218	            self.children = current_children[:-1]
   219	        else:
   220	            self.reset()
   221	
   222	        return self
   223	
   224	    def reset(self):
   225	        """
   226	        Empty the messages and hide it
   227	
   228	        Return:
   229	            self
   230	        """
   231	
   232	        self.children = [""]
   233	        self.hide()
   234	
   235	        return self

ln x * ln3 ln5 = ln(x +1) * ln2 ln5 + ln(x -3) * ln2 ln3

This is a transcendental equation and likely can't be solved algebraically. Therefore, x=8 is the only integer solution, and given the behavior of the functions, the only real solution.

Hence, the solution is x=8.
</think>To solve the equation \(\frac{\log x}{\log 2} - \frac{\log (x-3)}{\log 5} = \frac{\log (x+1)}{\log 3}\), we start by recognizing that each term can be rewritten using the change of base formula for logarithms. The equation can be rewritten as:
\[
\log_2 x - \log_5 (x-3) = \log_3 (x+1).
\]

We need to find a value of \(x\) that satisfies this equation. Let's test some integer values for \(x\):

1. **Testing \(x = 4\):**
   \[
   \log_2 4 = 2, \quad \log_5 (4-3) = \log_5 1 = 0, \quad \log_3 (4+1) = \log_3 5 \approx 1.465.
   \]
   So, the left side is \(2 - 0 = 2\) and the right side is approximately \(1.465\). They are not equal.

2. **Testing \(x = 5\):**
   \[
   \log_2 5 \approx 2.322, \quad \log_5 (5-3) = \log_5 2 \approx 0.431, \quad \log_3 (5+1) = \log_3 6 \approx 1.631.
   \]
   So, the left side is approximately \(2.322 - 0.431 = 1.891\) and the right side is approximately \(1.631\). They are not equal.

3. **Testing \(x = 8\):**
   \[
   \log_2 8 = 3, \quad \log_5 (8-3) = \log_5 5 = 1, \quad \log_3 (8+1) = \log_3 9 = 2.
   \]
   So, the left side is \(3 - 1 = 2\) and the right side is \(2\). They are equal.

To confirm that \(x = 8\) is the only solution, we analyze the behavior of the functions involved. As \(x\) approaches 3 from the right, \(\log_5 (x-3)\) approaches \(-\infty\), making the left side of the equation very large, while the right side remains finite. As \(x\) increases beyond 8, the left side grows slower than the right side because the logarithmic functions involved have different growth rates. Therefore, \(x = 8\) is the only solution.

Thus, the solution to the equation is:
\[
\boxed{8}
\]<#sep#>) must be in the unvisited set.

Thus, in code:

To compute:

start_x, start_y, start_z = current_position

for k in 1 to L:

   new_x = start_x + dx*k

   new_y = start_y + dy*k

   new_z = start_z + dz*k

   check (new_x, new_y, new_z) is within 1..N.

   also check if that position is in the visited set.

If any of these steps is out of bounds OR has been visited, this direction is not possible.

Only then can we proceed.

Additionally, after moving all L steps, the ending position becomes the new current_position.

We must add all the visited points along this path ( from start to start + L*direction) to the visited set.

Thus, in the backtracking function, whenever a direction is valid, we proceed to the next segment by:

- updating current_position to (start_x + L *dx, ... )

- adding to the visited set all the k steps positions.

Now, actually, in backtracking, the visited set must track all the prior positions. So, the order in backtracking is such that once a direction is chosen, all positions in that segment are *added* to the visited set (or represented as a set that get's copied for each recursive path), but this may be computationally prohibitive.

Hmm. To optimize, perhaps:

Instead of tracking a set for each recursive branch, which is slow and uses much memory, maybe represent the visited positions as a list and pass it as a parameter? Or use bitmask-based representation.

Alternatively, since the path is a contiguous path, the visited set for a given path at a segment is exactly the path taken so far. If you walk step by step, then maybe the path is always contiguous and the order is fixed, so perhaps the visited set can be represented as an array or a 3D array?

The nature of the path is each step adds consecutive elements, so each new segment's positions are exactly the new placed. So the bitmask can be updated as the path is built.

Thus, in code, the visited can be kept as an integer (like a bitmask), where the bits indicate which coordinates are already occupied. Let me see.

Let me think of how to encode the coordinates into an integer. For a 3D grid of N x N x N:

Each coordinate (x,y,z) can be mapped to a unique number. For example, coordinates (1,1,1) → index 0, (1,1,2) →1..., (x,y,z) → (x-1)*N*N + (y-1)*N + (z-1). 

But adding up the bits:

mask ^= (1 << (position_number)).

So for each position in the current path, set that bit to 1. To check if a position can be added, the bit must not be set.

Thus, the mask can be efficiently passed around in recursive steps, with O(1) checks. Then, for each step in choosing a direction, we can precompute the target positions along the new segment's path, check each of their bits in the mask, and compute the new mask if valid.

This is a crucial optimization that can make the backtracking approach feasible.

So, the steps would be:

For each direction candidate:

   compute the vector (dx, dy, d侵害, and length L, compute all the positions (x + k*dx, etc), which must be within 1..N for each coordinate.

   compute their index and check each of their bits are not set.

   Then, if all positions are safe, compute the new mask = mask | (all new bits). Also, compute new current position.
